(6)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[R-Can]}}\to

\displaystyle{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{res}}}}}}({x})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}

(7)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[C-Br]}}\to

\displaystyle{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\equiv{s}\lightning

(8)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}}_{6}[P_{6}]
for
Equation

1
is

P_{6}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{req}}}}}}({x})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {...}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}){.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}}}}

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}}_{9}[P_{9}]
for
Equation

5
is

P_{9}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{res}}}}}}\langle{\mathtt{d}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}}}}

both because of rule

[C-Cat]
.

Initially we reduce using the communication rule for
the branching and selection.
Next, we apply
[R-Com]
demonstrating how
the affine branching reduces under
\operatorname{\text{{try}}}
.
Then we apply
[T?Sel]
assuming an error
(or a timeout) occurs during the selection of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{res}}}
.
This generates a kill process
{s}\lightning
and spawns the
process in the
\operatorname{\text{{catch}}}
-block.

\operatorname{\text{{Cancel}}}
spawns a kill process
{s}\lightning
and hence reduces to
{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
,
following rule
[R-Can]
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}}=[\,]
.
Finally, applying
[R-Can]
cancels the linear selection.
To conclude, we garbage collect all kill processes.
Given that our initial parallel composition
has name restrictions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\right){}}
at the top level,
(omitted from the reduction for convenience), we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\right){{s}\lightning}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

Example 3.7

(
Rules

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[C-?Sel]}}/{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[C-Sel]}}

and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[C-?Br]}}/{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{\scriptsize[C-Br]}}

)
.

We can obtain the reduction

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{r}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s^{\prime}}\lightning
, by first
deriving
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{r}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{r}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning=R
by rule
[C-?Sel]
,
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{r}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s}\lightning\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{s^{\prime}}\lightning
by rule
[C-Sel]
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
contains some prefix at s.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
can receive a signal from
{s}\lightning
and can fail
before an action from

A.1
to
A.1
.
However, this decomposition into two rules allows

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[R]}
to interact with other processes in the context

\mathbb{C}
before generating
{s^{\prime}}\lightning
,
capturing a time difference (nondeterministic failures/asynchrony).

3.2
Affine multiparty session typing system

Global and local types

The advantage of affine session frameworks is that no change
of the syntax of types
from the original system is required.
We follow
[
53
]
which is the most widely used syntax in the
literature.
A
global type
, written
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}^{\prime},\dots
,
describes the whole
conversation scenario of a multiparty session as a type signature, and
a
local type
, written by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}^{\prime},\dots
,
represents a local protocol for each participant.
The syntax of types is given as:

Definition 3.8

(Global types)
.

The syntax of a
global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,\Coloneqq\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{i}}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})\vphantom{x}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\neq\!{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
I\!\neq\!\emptyset
,  and
\forall i\!\in\!I:\operatorname{fv}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}}\right)=\emptyset

The syntax of
local types
is:

\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}\,\Coloneqq\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}\quad\text{with $I\!\neq\!\emptyset$,
and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}$ pairwise distinct.}

Types must be closed, and recursion variables to be guarded.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}\in\mathbb{A}
corresponds to the usual message labels in the session type
theory.
Global branching type

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{i}}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})\vphantom{x}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}

states that participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
can
send a message with one of the
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{i}}
labels and a
message payload type

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}

to the participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
and that interaction
described in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}
follows.
We require
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
to prevent self-sent messages and

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{i}}\not={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{k}}
for all
i\not=k\in J
. Recursive types

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
are for recursive protocols,
assuming those type variables (
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}^{\prime},\dots
) are
guarded in the
standard way, i.e., they only occur under branching.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}
represents session termination (often omitted).
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
(or simply
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
)  iff, for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}
, either
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}{\to}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}{\to}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
occurs in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
.
The function
\mathsf{id}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G})
gives the participants of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
.

For local types, the
branching type

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}

specifies the reception of a message from

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
with a label among the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}
and a payload
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}
. The
selection type

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}

is its
dual
– its opposite operation.
The remaining type constructors are as for global types.
We say a type is
guarded
if it is neither a recursive type nor a type variable.

Projection

The relation between global and local types is formalised by
projection
[
11
,
27
]
.

Definition 3.9

(Projection)
.

The
projection of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}

(written
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
) is defined as:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{i}}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})\vphantom{x}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}\right)}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\;=\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 0.5mm}l@{\hskip 5mm}l@{}}1.42262pt\lx@intercol\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}})}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{\footnotesize if\, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}$}\\
1.42262pt\lx@intercol\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}})}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{\footnotesize if\, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}$}\\
1.42262pt\lx@intercol{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\bigsqcap_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{\footnotesize if\, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}$}\end{array}\right.\\[17.07164pt]
\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}})}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 0.5mm}l@{\hskip 5mm}l@{}}1.42262pt\lx@intercol{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}})}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{\footnotesize if\, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$ or $\operatorname{fv}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right)\not=\emptyset$}\\
1.42262pt\lx@intercol{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\text{\footnotesize otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}\end{array}\end{array}

For projection of branching, we introduce a merge
operator along the lines of
[
14
]
,
written
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}^{\prime}
, ensuring
that if the locally observable behaviour of the local type is
dependent of the chosen branch then it is identifiable via a unique
choice/branching label.

The
merging operation

\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}{}{}
is defined as a partial commutative
operator over two types such that:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\textstyle\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\,\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{\!j\in J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{\!j}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{j}}}}\;=\;\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{k\in I\cap J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{k}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{k}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{k}}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{k}})}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&}}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I\setminus J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&}}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{\!j\in J\setminus I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{\!j}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{j}}}}\\[2.84526pt]
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\,\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\;=\;\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}}}\,\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T})}}\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}\,\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\sqcap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T}\end{array}

As an example of projection, consider

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{A}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{B}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{a}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{B}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{b}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{A}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{c}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}}}}\right\}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{d}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{B}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{e}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{A}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{f}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}}}}\ .
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
’s local type is given by:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{B}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{b}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{A}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{c}}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{e}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{A}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{f}}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}\}}}}}
.
We say that
G
is
well-formed
if for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\!\mathscr{P}\!
,

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is defined.

.

Definition 3.10

(Subtyping)
.

The subtyping relation
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\leqslant}}
is
co
inductively defined as:

\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{i\in I\cup J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{i}}}}\lx@proof@logical@and\forall J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\leqslant}}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in We define typing contexts which are used to define properties of
type-level behaviours.

Definition 3.11

(Typing contexts)
.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}
denotes a partial mapping from process variables to
n
-tuples of types, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
denotes a partial mapping from channels to types, defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}\;\;\Coloneqq\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{n}}}}\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\,\Coloneqq\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}}}

The
composition

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{1}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{2}}

is defined iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{1}}}\right)}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{2}}}\right)}=\emptyset
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\!\not\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
iff
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}\!\not\in\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}
(i.e., session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
does not occur in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
).We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}\!=\!\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\right\}}}
iff
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}
there is
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}
(i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
only contains session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
); and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\leqslant}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\right)}
and
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\leqslant}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\right)}
.

Next, we define typing context properties defined by its reduction.

Definition 3.12

(
Typing context reduction,
safety,
deadlock-freedom and liveness
)
.

•

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
with

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{0}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&_{j\in J}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T_{j}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{j}}}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{0}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}T^{\prime}_{j}}}}
where types are defined modulo unfolding recursive types.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
for a transitive and
reflexive closure of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}
; and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}
if
there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

•

\varphi
is a
safety property
of typing contexts iff:

[S-
{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\oplus}}}{\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\&}}}
]

Definition 3.13

(Typing judgement)
.

The typing judgement for processes has the form:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\ \text{(with ${\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}$/${\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}$ omitted when empty)}

(9)

and are defined by the typing rules in
Figure

4
with
the judgements for process variables and channels.
For convenience, we type-annotate channels bound by process definitions and restrictions.
Note that
\operatorname{end}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
denotes that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
only contains
type
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}
.

Figure 4:
Multiparty session typing rules.
We highlight
the new rules
from
[
53
]
.

We explain each rule highlighting the new rules from
[
53
]
.

(Affine) Branching/Selection

[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&}
]
and
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oplus}
]
are the
standard rules for branching and selection, which can also type
affine branching and selection.
Note that
the premise

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y_{i}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S^{\prime}_{i}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}
in
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&}
]
ensures that
selecting one branch in the reduction rule defined by

[C-Br]
is
sufficient for ensuring type soundness.

Try-Catch and Cancellation

[T-try]
is typing a try process: we ensure

P
has a unique subject and catch block process
Q
has
the same session typing (similar with branching).

[T-cancel]
generates a kill process at its
declared session.

Kill process

[T-kill]
types
a kill process that appears
during reductions: the cancellation of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}
is broadcasting the cancellation
to all processes which belong to session
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.

Recursions

[T-def]
and
[T-call]

are identical to those of
[
53
]
.

Restriction

Processes are initially typed projecting a global type by
[T-init]
,
while running processes are typed by
[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\nu}}
]

(see the proof of Theorem
3.16
).

(Typing
AMPST
processes)
.

process from the reduction example.

3.3
Properties of affine multiparty session types

This subsection proves the main properties of
AMPST
processes.
We first prove basic properties such as Subject Congruence and
Reduction Theorems, then prove important properties,
session fidelity, deadlock-freedom and liveness.
The highlight is cancellation termination, which guarantees
that once an exceptional behaviour is triggered,
all parties in a single session can terminate as nil processes.

Unlike linear-logic based typing systems

[
45
]
, we do
not

assume that the typing system is closed modulo

\equiv
. Instead, we prove closedness of
\equiv
for
tricky cases, e.g., kill and try-catches.

Theorem 3.15

(Subject Congruence)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\equiv\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, then
we have

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
.

By Theorem
3.15
,

AMPST
processes satisfy
type soundness
.

Theorem 3.16

(Subject Reduction)
.

Suppose

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
safe.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}

implies there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
is safe and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Theta}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
While the syntax of types stays the
same as
[
53
]
,
the proof needs extra care
due to reduction semantics not presented in
[
53
]
.
∎

A single agent in a multiparty session
s
is a
participant playing a single role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
s
.
We use the definition from
[
53
]
except the
highlighted part, which now includes affine processes.

Definition 3.17

(A unique role process)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
:

1.

has guarded definitions
iff  in each subterm of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{def}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{1}}}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{n}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{n}}}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}\;\operatorname{\text{{in}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
,  for all
i\in 1..n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S_{i}}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\not\leqslant}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}
implies  that a call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}\!\left\langle{\dots,x_{i},\dots}\right\rangle}
can only occur in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
as subterm of
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\dagger}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x_{i}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\sum_{j\in J}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{j}}}}}}({y_{j}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{j}}}}}
or
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\dagger}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x_{i}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}}}}}
(i.e., after using
x_{i}
for selection/branching);

2.

only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
​,
iff:

i)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
has guarded definitions;

ii)

\operatorname{fv}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\right)\!=\!\emptyset
;

iii)

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{0}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1},}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}S}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\not\leqslant}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}
and
\operatorname{end}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{0}}})
;

iv)

in all subterms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}^{\prime}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathbf{end}}}}
(for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}^{\prime}}
).

We say “
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
only plays role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
”  iff
\exists{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}:{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\!}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, and item
2
holds.

Note that by definition, a unique role process in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
includes

{s}\lightning
.

Session fidelity
is an important property to ensure
liveness and deadlock-freedom, as well as termination.
We extend that in
[
53
]
by taking a kill process into account.
A set of unique role processes of a single multiparty session,
together with kill processes always make progress if
a typing context has progress,
satisfying a protocol compliance.

Below we write
{Q}\lightning
if
Q
contains only a parallel composition
of kill processes.

Theorem 3.18

(Session Fidelity)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
is safe,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{Q}\lightning
,  and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{0}}

such that, for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
;
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}_{0}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{Q}\lightning}}
.
Assume that each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Then,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\!\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}
implies
\exists{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}\!\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\to}}\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
,  with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}
safe,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning
,  and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}

such that, for each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
,
and each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, or only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
;
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}\!}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma^{\prime}}_{0}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\lightning}}
.

By the above theorem, we can prove deadlock-freedom and liveness for
a single session multiparty session in the presence of
affine processes.

Definition 3.19

(Deadlock-freedom and liveness)
.

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is
deadlock-free
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}\!\not{\to}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\!\equiv\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

2.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is
live
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\!\equiv\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}}\!\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\right]
implies:

i)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}}}
(for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}
),  then
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}}\!\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\right]
;  and

ii)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{i}}}}}
(for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{i}}
),  then
\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}},k\!\in\!I,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}}\!\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}_{k}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}}{{x_{k}}}}\right\}}}}\right]
.

Note that liveness is defined for

linear
selection or
linear
branching
processes
which appear at the top level, i.e., under the
reduction context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{\ }
,
not under
try
-
catch
construct, cancel nor affine branching and selection
processes.

Theorem 3.20

(Deadlock-freedom)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
,
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
safe,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
,
each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
, or only playing role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.
Then,
\operatorname{df}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})

implies that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
with
\{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}

is deadlock-free.

Proof.

We use Theorem
3.18
.
See
Appendix

A
. ∎

As discussed in
[
53
, Definition 5.11]
,
we require
\operatorname{live^{+}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
for proving
liveness.

Theorem 3.21

(Liveness)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\emptyset}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}
,
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}
safe,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\equiv\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in I}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
,
each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
,
or only playing role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
.
Then,
\operatorname{live^{+}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
implies that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is live.

Proof.

We use Theorem
3.18
.
See
Appendix

A
. ∎

Now we consider a user-written Rust program with one session as
an
initial program
.

Definition 3.22

(Initial program)
.

We say

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is an
initial program

if

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\mathbf{\nu}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}}}\right){\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}}
with
\{\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}\}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}}\right)}
;

2.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
only plays
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}
;

3.

in each subterm of the form,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{\text{{def}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}(\widetilde{x})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}\;\operatorname{\text{{in}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}}}
,
(1)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
is of the form
\operatorname{\text{{try}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}}\ \operatorname{\text{{catch}}}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}}}
;
and (2)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime\prime}}
does not contain any (free or bound) process call.

4.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}=\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\right\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}^{\prime}

for some
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
and
\operatorname{end}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}^{\prime}})
;

5.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is derived
using
[T-init]

instead of

[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\nu}}
]
; and
without
[T-kill]
.

Condition (3) ensures that once a process moves to the

catch-block
, then it ensures finite computation; (4,5) state
that the initial program starts conforming to a global protocol.

Remark 3.23

(Initial processes)
.

Condition (3) does not limit the expressiveness since
the
try-block
can include infinite computations; and
conditions (4,5) imply that
an initial program typed by
condition (1) has started.
Notice that running (runtime) processes generated from the initial program
are typed using

[T-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\nu}}
]
and
[T-kill]
;
hence the proof of the subject reduction holds with

Lemma

3.24
below.

Before proving the main theorems, we state that a set of local types
projected from a well-formed global type satisfy the safety property.

Lemma 3.24

(
[
L
]
emma 5.9)
.

scalasLess2019]

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}{\upharpoonright}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}}}}
.
Then
\operatorname{safe}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
,
\operatorname{df}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
and

\operatorname{live^{+}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\Gamma}})
.

Now we state the two main theorems of this paper:
deadlock-freedom, liveness and
cancellation termination.
The cancellation termination theorem states that
once a kill signal is produced by
cancellation or affine processes
(due to a timeout or an
error), then all processes are enabled to terminate.
We start from deadlock-freedom.

Corollary 3.25

(Deadlock-freedom for an initial program)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is an initial program.
Then for all
P
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}

is deadlock-free.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.20

and
Lemma

3.24
.
∎

Corollary 3.26

(Liveness for an initial program)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is an initial program.
Then for all
P
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}

is live.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.21

and
Lemma

3.24
.
∎

Theorem 3.27

(Cancellation Termination)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is an initial program.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[{s}\lightning]}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}
,
then we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
. We use Corollary
3.25
.
∎

Corollary 3.28

(Cancellation Termination of Affine and Cancel Processes)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}
is an initial program.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[{\operatorname{\text{{cancel}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}){.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}^{\prime}}}}]}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}
,
then we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}}{}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\sum_{i\in I}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}}}}({x_{i}})\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}}}}]}]}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\!\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}}{}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textbf{?}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}]}}[\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}]\mathbin{\!\oplus\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}}}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}^{\prime}}[{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}]}}\rangle\vphantom{x}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}]}]}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}
,
then we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\to{}^{\!\!\!*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

Proof.

By
[C-?Sel]
,

[T?Sel]
,

[C-?Br]
,

[T?Br]
,
and
[R-Can]
, we have

P^{\prime}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}{}{[{s}\lightning]}
.
Then we apply Theorem
3.27
.
∎

Remark 3.29

(Termination theorem)
.

The cancellation termination theorem
means that
there always exists a path
which leads to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
;
and an initial program might not terminate even if it contains
a process with
{s}\lightning
.
This differs from the total termination, i.e., all paths are finite –
a program will definitely stop as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.
However, if we apply
fair traversal sets
, i.e., fair scheduling,
from
[
53
, Definition 5.5]
, applying to processes
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}}{}{[{s}\lightning]}
, we can prove the total termination.
Since these extensions require an introduction of labelled transition
systems for processes, we leave it as future work.

4
Design and implementation of
MultiCrusty

4.1
Challenges for the implementation of
MultiCrusty

The three main challenges underpinning the implementation of

AMPST
in Rust are related to multiparty
communications and ensuring correctness for affine channels.

(Challenge 1) Realising a multiparty channel by binary channels.

AMPST
relies on a
multiparty channel
– a channel that can communicate with
several roles.
In Rust, communication channels are peer-to-peer,
e.g., they are
binary

[
36
]
.
To overcome this limitation, we extend an encoding of
MPST
into binary channels
[
51
]
.
In this encoding, a multiparty channel
can be represented as an
indexed tuple
of
one-shot binary channels

used in a sequence depending on the ordering specified by the type.
This design ensures reception error safety by construction.
Since each pair of binary channels is dual, then no communication mismatch can occur.
We piggyback on this result by introducing meshed channels,
which reuse an existing library of binary session types in
Rust
[
36
]
with built-in duality guarantees.
We explain the implementation of meshed channels
in §
4.2
.
notably
distributed logging
(
Figure

16
),
and
circuit breaker
(
Figure

17
).

(Challenge 2) Deadlock-freedom, liveness and termination.

Duality is unfortunately insufficient to guarantee deadlock-freedom.
The naive decomposition of binary channels leads to

hard to
detect deadlock errors
[
51
]
.
To ensure liveness properties and correct termination
of cancellation behaviour, we integrate
MultiCrusty
with
two state-of-the-art
verification toolchains – Scribble
[
33
]

and
k
-MC
[
41
]
,
that ensures meshed channel types are
correct
.
The former generates correct meshed channel types in Rust,
while the latter verifies a set of existing meshed channel types.
In both cases, well-typed processes implemented using well-typed meshed channels
are free from deadlocks, orphan messages and reception errors.
We display the Rust types for our running example
in §
B.3
.

(Challenge 3) Affinity with
try
-
catch
and optional types.
Rust does not have a native
try
-
catch
construct,
but macros and optional types.
We use them to design and implement
a
try
-
catch
block and affine selection and branching.
Channels can be implicitly or explicitly cancelled,
and all processes are
guaranteed to terminate gracefully
in the event of a cancellation,
avoiding endless cascading errors.
We discuss our design choices in §
4.4
.

4.2
Meshed Channels in
MultiCrusty

⬇

1

pub

struct

MeshedChannels
<

S1
:

Session
,

S2
:

Session
,

R
:

Role
,

N
:

Role
>

{

2

pub

session1
:

S1
,

pub

session2
:

S
,

pub

stack
:

R
,

pub

name
:

N

}

Figure 5
:
Generated MeshedChannles structure.

A multiparty channel in
MultiCrusty
is realised as an
affine meshed channel
(hereafter meshed channel),
which has three ingredients:
(1) a list of separate binary channels (one binary channel for each pair of
participants); (2) a stack that imposes the ordering between the binary channels;
and (3) the name of the role, whose behaviour is implemented by the meshed channel.
For example, a meshed channel for a 3-party protocol can be generated using the macro

gen_mpst!
(
MeshedChannels
,

A
,

C
,

S
)
:
the name of the structure (
MeshedChannels
),
and the three roles involved in
the communication (
A
,

C
,

S
).

Figure

5

shows the generated structure.

The generated structure,
MeshedChannels
, holds four fields.
The first two fields,
session1
and
session2
, are of type
Session
which
is a binary session type. Therefore, these fields store binary channels.

Session
in Rust is a
trait

and a
trait
is similar to an interface.
The
Session
trait can be instantiated to three generic (binary session) types:
an
End
type; a
Recv
<
T
,

S
>
or a
Send
<
T
,

S
>
type,
with their respective payload of type
T
and their continuation of a binary session type
S
.
This has important implications for the design and safety of our system.
Since all pairs of binary channels
are created and distributed across meshed channels at the start of the protocol,
the binary type
Session
enforces that each pair of binary channels are dual.
For example, the binary channel for role
S
inside the meshed channels for role
A
;
and the binary channel for role
A
inside
the meshed channels for role
S
are dual.
This design ensures that, without using any external tools, our system is communication safe,
no reception error can occur. This is insufficient to guarantee deadlock-freedom,
which is why we utilise Scribble or bounded model checking, i.e.,
k
-MC, as an additional verification step.

The rest of the fields of the
struct

MeshedChannels
are stack-like structures,
stack
and
name
, which represent
respectively the order of the interactions (in what order the binary channels should be used)
and the associated role.
For instance, the behaviour where role
A
has to communicate first with role
S
,
then with role
C
and then the session ends, can be specified using a
stack of type
RoleS
<
RoleC
<
RoleEnd
>>
. Note that all stack
types such as
RoleS
and
RoleC
are generated singleton types.
Role names are codified as
RoleX
<
RoleEnd
>
where
X
is the actual
name of the participant. For instance, role
A
is realised as the singleton type
RoleA
<
RoleEnd
>
.
We chose this design for its readability and its ease of implementation:
one can guess at a glance the current state of a participant.

The code generation macro
gen_mpst!

produces
meshed channels for any finite number of communicating processes
.
For example, in the case of a protocol with four roles, the macro

gen_mpst!
will generate a meshed channel with five
fields – one field for the binary session between each pair of
participants (which is 3 fields in total), one field for the stack and one field for the name of the role that is being implemented.

4.3
Types for affine meshed channels

Meshed channel types –
MeshedChannels
– correspond
to local session types.
They describe the behaviour of each meshed channel
and specify which communication primitives
are permitted on a meshed channel.
To better illustrate meshed channel types, we explain the type
RecA
<
N
>
for
role
A
(Authenticator) from
Figure

2(b)
.
The types are displayed in
Figure

15
.
The types of the meshed channels for the other roles,
i.e.,
C
and
S
are available
in
Figures

12
and

of the Appendix.

Figure 6
:
Local Rust types for role

(Authenticator) from
